```text
### Cell 0 markdown
# **Module 3 &mdash; Linear Regression, Gradient Descent and Feature Engineering**

### Cell 1 markdown
## **3.1  Parametric Learning<font color='#FFCC00'>  &#x2B24; </font>**

We now consider a simple supervised regression problem. The data matrix $X$ has dimension $n\times 1$ and the labels matrix $y$, also of dimension $n\times 1$ contains numerical entries. These pairs $(X_i,y_i)$ are visualized in the following picture. This is a very special 1-dimensional example that we will generalize later.  

![Noisy linear function](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1rS4LqE73cYBlVipHusmsQJL05Y3zdMD9)


**Goal.** We want to find a relatively simple and concise 'law', i.e. a function $y=f(x)$, that describes the data as well as possible. Recall that an 1-NN algorithm can always return precisely the correct label for each point $X_i$ in the given data set $X$. In this case, because we want the function to be relatively simple, it is unlikely that we can find an $f$ such that $y_i$ is exactly equal to $f(X_i)$ for all points $X_i$. We will instead aim to find a function such that $f(X_i)$ is - in some sense - a *good approximation* to $y_i$. The intuition is that such a function $f$ can then used to give predictions for the numerical labels of unseen points.

**Modeling the data.** Data often contains some natural variability, possibly due to measurement noise etc. The given data appear to approximately follow a line with some added variability. So, we can use our intuition and *model* the data as a line; that is, we assume that the function is the form of a line:
$$
   y = ax+b\, .
$$

Here $a,b$ are unknown parameters. The equation $y = ax+b$ is thus a set of infinitely many *hypotheses*, or *models* for the 'law' that generates data, and we want to find values for $a$ and $b$ that give the best possible hypothesis or *model* - that is, the model that gives the best approximations $f(X_i)$ for $y_i$.

Data modeling has its origins in Statistics. Indeed, Machine Learning is historically rooted in Statistics. The material of this section predates Machine Learning by many decades, or even centuries. But it is in the core of nearly all algorithms in current ML and Deep Learning.




### Cell 2 markdown
## **3.2 Loss Function<font color='#FF5733'> &#x2B24; </font>**

Suppose we have now fixed a model $f$. It is not too difficult to quantify the error of approximation on a single data point $X_i$:
$$
    e_i = |f(X_i) - y_i|
$$

We can extend these individual errors to a global error, that we will call the *loss function*, defined as follows:
$$
    L = \frac{1}{n} \cdot\sum_{i=1}^n e_i^2
$$

With this definition, $L$ is often called the ***mean squared error***. When $L$ is small we expect that most individual errors $e_i$ will also be small. So, it makes sense to find a model $f(x) = ax + b$ that yields a small loss $L$. Recall that $X$ and $y$ are given to us, so $L$ only depends on the choice of $a,b$. We will sometimes write $L(a,b)$ to emphasize that.







### Cell 3 markdown
### **3.2.1 Why MSE?**

There are potentially many ways to define a global loss. For example, we could have picked the much simpler function $L = (1/n) \sum_i e_i$, or even
$L = max_i\{e_i\}$. MSE is a popular choice, though, mainly due to its nice mathematical properties.

~![Convex function illustration](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1zfYT544LpWnnYN-IDPB1zAVXMDuHVTq3)

This 3D plot demonstrates these properties. Here the two axes correspond to the variables $a,b$ and the third axis corresponds to $L(a,b)$. Its characteristic shape illustrates that the function is convex, and as such, it has a unique local and global minimum. In other words, there is a unique setting of $(a,b)$ that minimize the loss. A general way to think about the minimum is this: For each value $c>0$ there is a set of points $(a,b)$ that satisfy $L(a,b)=c$, and that set is called a function level set - in the above 3D plot all points in a level set get precisely the same color. The level set for $c=0$ is a unique point.

The function is also smooth, or 'differentiable' at any point $(a,b)$. And that brings us to calculus.

### Cell 4 markdown
## **3.3 Gradient Descent in a Nutshell<font color='#FF5733'> &#x2B24; </font>**

### Cell 5 markdown
### **3.3.1 Gradient Descent in One Variable**

Consider a simple function with one variable $y=x^2$ (by the way, these $x,y$ have nothing to do with our model $y=ax+b$, we are just reusing sumbols).

![Quadratic Function](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1rSz4bISQVNgtHGhFQyJnh8_pk0j39P66)

You may recall from calculus that the derivative of this function is $f'(x) = 2x$. By its definition, the derivative at some point $x$ gives us the rate at which the function changes. Moreover, the sign of the derivative tells us whether the function is locally increasing. As an example, at point $x=2$ we get a positive derivative of 4, which means that the function is increasing as $x$ increases. On the other hand, the derivative at $x=-2$ is equal to $-4$, which means that the function is decreasing.

If we then want to ***nudge*** our point $x$ towards the minimum of the function we can do an update of the following form:
$$
     x \leftarrow x - \rho \cdot \mathit{sign}\,(f'(x))\, ,
$$
where $\rho$ is a step size. We can carefully check and see that independent of whether the derivative has a positive or negative sign, if $\rho$ is sufficiently small, the updated $x$ will be closer to the minimum of the function.

A good $\rho$ should depend on how fast the function changes, i.e. in the derivative itself. It is then more natural to make $\rho$ independent of the derivative and use the following update rule:
$$
     x \leftarrow x - \rho \cdot f'(x)\, .
$$

This update steps can be *repeated* multiple times to bring our point $x$ closer to the minimum. Because these iterative updates nudge our point to descend towards the minimum, and we use the derivative (the gradient for one dimension), we call this update process ***gradient descent***.

**Exercise:** Think about the effect of picking a step size $\rho<1$, or $\rho>1$, or $\rho=1$.

### Cell 6 markdown
### **3.3.2 Partial Derivatives**

Consider now a 2-variate function, e.g. $f(a,b) = a^2+3ab+b^2+3$.
One can use partial derivatives to get information about the speed of the function in each coordinate/axis separately.

For example, we can consider $b$ as a fixed number, and compute the partial derivative of $f$ with respect to $a$:

$$
    \frac{\partial f }{\partial a} = 2a + 3b \, .
$$

### Cell 7 markdown
### **3.3.3 Gradient Descent in Multiple Dimensions**

Suppose now we have a fixed point $(a,b)$ and we want to nudge it towards a minimum of the function $f(x,y)$. This can done by finding the update rule along the $x$-axis only, as if $b$ was fixed, and vice-versa. This gives us two
different single-variable update rules:

$$
a \leftarrow a - \rho\,\frac{\partial f}{\partial a}\, ,
### Cell 0 markdown
# **Module 6 &mdash; Regularization**

### Cell 1 markdown
## **6.1 Sensitivity to Noise<font color='#FFCC00'> &#x2B24; </font>**

Consider as a visual example the following 2D dataset.

![2D dataset with 3 classes](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=17Wdo7zfVOBjL5qLtkmpZBRKPfmzAewDH)

Suppose now we have one point $x=(x_1,x_2)$, and a second one $x' = (x_1+\epsilon,x_2)$, which comes from adding a bit of noise $\epsilon$ to $x_1$.
Since we expect that nearby points in space should belong to the same class, the classifier should not be too sensitive to the noise.

Let's then consider the net input of a classifier with weights $[w_0,w_1,w_2]$ for our two points:

* $z = w_1 x_1 + w_2 x_2 + w_0$, &nbsp; and
* $z' \: = \: w_1(x_1+\epsilon) + w_2x_2 + w_0 \: = \: z + w_1\epsilon$.

Intuitively, if $w_1$ is large, then even a small amount of noise $\epsilon$  will cause a large change in the net input $z$.

From this we can see that smaller values of the parameters $w_i$ would be preferred, since they may lead to better generalization. That leads us to regularization, a class of techniques meant to fight overfitting.



### Cell 2 markdown
## **6.2 Regularization<font color='#008DFF'> &#x2B24; </font>**

We would like to impose a penalty to large values for the parameters $w$. This can be done by adding a penalty to the loss function. More specifically, if our loss function is $L$, we can then define a new loss function
$$
    L_{\mathit{reg}}(w) = L(w) + \lambda\,\|w\|_2^2 \, ,
$$
where $\lambda$ is some hyperparameter that controls the strength of regularization.
This new loss function imposes a penalty to having a large 2-norm of the weights, which is the reason it is called $l_2$-regularization. Note that if $\lambda$ is large, the optimization favors those choices of $w$ for which $\|w\|_2$ is smaller, which in turn forces smaller values for the individual weights $w_i$. Also note that some applications may use the unsquared 2-norm,
$$
    L_{\mathit{reg}}(w) = L(w) + \lambda\,\|w\|_2 \, ,
$$
which penalizes the growth in vector norm linearly rather than quadratically. (With the learning algorithms covered in this course, the squared 2-norm is more commonly used.)

Alternatively, we can perform $l_1$-regularization by defining this loss function as
$$
    L_{\mathit{reg}}(w) = L(w) + \lambda\,\|w\|_1 \, .
$$

Regularization can also be applied to linear regression, where $l_2$ and $l_1$ regularization are known as *Ridge* and *Lasso* regression, respectively. A mix of $l_2$ and $l_1$ regularization that adds two different regularization terms is known as *elastic nets*.

These new loss functions penalize for large weights and intuitively will make it harder for the line to be 'dragged' by one outlier; hence, the model will be less prone to overfitting. These loss functions can be optimized with an appropriate optimizer. Usually a standard optimizer can treat  (via gradient descent) the $l_2$ regularization without any difficulties, but optimizing with $l_1$-regularization is a harder computational problem.





### Cell 3 markdown
## **6.3 Geometric Understanding of Regularization<font color='#FFCC00'> &#x2B24; </font>**



![Landscape of a Convex Function](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1ERFb77r-ogUpDrAddLok5VoJ2t0cKlL9)

Consider the pictured landscape of a loss function of a two dimensional model, where the $z$-axis is the value of the loss. Pairs $(x,y)$ that give the same loss value $L$ are plotted with the same color at height $z=L$. These contours are called a **level set** of the function. Level sets form closed curves if we 'project' them onto the 2D plane.


### Cell 4 markdown
Let's now consider the $l_2$-regularization loss function $L_{\mathit{reg}}(w) = L(w) + \lambda\,\|w\|_2^2$. This is a sum of two different functions: a loss (or 'cost') $L(w)$, and a penalty $\lambda\,\|w\|_2$. The following image shows the contour plots of both functions in a simple 2D example. The contour plot of the regularization part consists of circles, because the set of points that are on the same level $L$ define the circle $w_1^2+w_2^2=L$.

![Contour plots for l2-regularization](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1ERqBeXKfJ0m56rdqTzR0gGnH1MicNZoj)

There are two different points that minimize each part separately, but their sum is minimized at a different intermediate point. In fact, in the case of $l_2$-regression, there is a unique tangent line to the corresponding contour curves of the two functions.

### Cell 5 markdown
Now consider the $l_1$-loss regularization $L_{reg}(w) = L(w) + \lambda\,\|w\|_1$
and the corresponding contour plots.

<img src='https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1ESQOBvxxjtolKx0qtLVLMHts0kMjfMS1' width=400 height=320>

In this case, the contour plots of the regularization term are squares around the origin, since the equations $|w_1|+|w_2|=L$ define squares.  Again, the sum of the two functions is minimized at an intermediate point.

$l_1$-regularization has a very interesting characteristic. Because the loss function $L(w)$ is convex, and $\|w\|_1$ has square-like contours, it is very likely that the corners of $\|w\|_1$ will penetrate deeper towards the center of the convex contours of the loss function. Intuitively, the sum of the two functions will be minimized at these corners of $\|w\|_1$ (even in higher dimensions), i.e. at points where many weights $w_j$ are equal to 0. In the picture we see that the minimium occurs at $w_1=0$.

Thus, whenever we do $l_1$ regularization the solution vector $w$ is likely to have multiple zero entries. This may imply that the corresponding inputs/attributes are not as important as the others (e.g. if $w_1=0$, then the feature value $x_1$ may not so relevant). Thus we can use $l_1$ regularization as a means of determining those features that are more 'relevant' or 'impactful'.



### Cell 0 markdown
# **Module 8 &mdash; Decision Trees**

### Cell 1 markdown
## **8.1  What Is a Decision Tree?<font color='#FFCC00'> &#x2B24; </font>**

![A categorical dataset](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1z03E38is-uVRjd06SOtNtCkQaofNFsvv)

Consider the dataset shown above. It consists of 14 'points'; each point has 4 categorical attributes, and a label (yes/no).

When dealing with such categorical datasets, we may want to come up with a set of rules, or a 'protocol of questions' that will classify a new point. More concretely, we would like to come up with a tree of branching questions and decisions like this:

![A decision tree](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1z0Q2Y5vDTyQ56SjO0TqjM3G6tfvHeeD_)

Using this tree, we could first ask about the weather 'Outlook'. If it were 'Sunny', we could then ask about the 'Humidity', and if that were 'Normal', then our decision would be 'Yes'.

This is an example of what we call a *decision tree*. This type of structure is called a 'tree' since if we turn it upside-down, it looks like one. Its 'leaves' are labeled with a classification decision. In this example, the classification is binary, but we can have multiple output classes as well.

**Note:** This is a very small example, one which does not reveal all the characteristics that are common to larger decision trees. Here, the first question attribute 'Outlook' appears only once (at the the root of the tree), but other attributes could appear more than once in the tree. For example, in a larger dataset the  'Humidity' attribute could appear in many branches, and not necessarily all at the same level of the tree.






### Cell 2 markdown
#### **Interpretability**

Decision trees are an interesting algorithm, since they have been designed to provide an explanation for each decision. This might be  particularly desirable in applications such as medicine, where a physician must follow a diagnostic protocol that progressively narrows down the possibilities and issues a diagnosis (which for us is simply a 'label' for a set of features).  

### Cell 3 markdown
## **8.2 Which Question to Ask First?<font color='#008DFF'> &#x2B24; </font>**

We now want to design an algorithm that will learn a 'good' decision tree for a categorical dataset, like the 'PlayedFootball' dataset shown above. Here we will use our intuition and introduce the following learning bias: a good question is one that will allow us to gain as much information as possible about our point.

We will then need to use a measure for quantifying the information gain. The notion of **entropy** is known to capture information well.

### **8.2.1 Entropy of a Probability Distribution<font color='#FF5733'> &#x2B24; </font>**

Given a probability distribution $d = [p_1,\ldots,p_k]$, where $p_i\geq 0$ and $\sum_i p_i = 1$, the entropy of $p$ is defined as follows:

$$
    \mathit{entropy}\,(d) = -\sum_{i=1}^k p_i \log_2 p_i \, .
$$

### **8.2.2 Entropy of a Dataset<font color='#FF5733'> &#x2B24; </font>**

We now consider again our favorite dataset:

![A categorical dataset](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1z03E38is-uVRjd06SOtNtCkQaofNFsvv)

We can see that we have 9 'Yes' points and 5 'No' points. Under the assumption that each situation is equally likely, this gives a probability distribution for the labels, $[9/14, 5/14]$.

We can then define the entropy of the dataset, as the entropy of the distribution of its labels, which in this case is $- \frac{9}{14}\log_2 \frac{9}{14} - \frac{5}{14}\log_2 \frac{5}{14}$.

The smallest possible value for the entropy is 0, when one of the probabilities $p_i$ in the distribution $d$ is equal to 1. That is precisely the case when all points of the dataset will have the same label, in which case we don't even have to ask any questions!















### Cell 4 markdown
### **8.2.3 Entropy of a Feature<font color='#FF5733'> &#x2B24; </font>**

Let's consider as an example the 'Wind' feature. This has two possible values, 'Weak' and 'Strong'. This means that the 'Wind' feature subdivides our dataset $X$ into two disjoint datasets $X_{\mathit{wind = weak}}$ and $X_{\mathit{wind = strong}}$. We can then compute the entropies of these two datasets.

$X_{\mathit{wind = weak}}$: <br>
We have 8 days with a weak wind, and for 6 of them the answer is 'Yes'. This gives the probability distribution $d_1 = [6/8, 2/8]$, and
from that we can calculate $\mathit{entropy}\,(d_1)$.

$X_{\mathit{wind = strong}}$: <br>
We have 6 days with a strong wind, and for 3 of them the answer is 'Yes'. This gives the probability distribution $d_1 = [3/6, 3/6]$ and from that we can calculate $\mathit{entropy}\,(d_2)$.

We can then define the entropy of the 'Wind' attribute as a weighted average of these two subdataset entropies, where the weights are given by the relative sizes of the two data subsets.

$$
   \mathit{entropy}\,(\mathrm{Wind}) = \frac{8}{14} \mathit{entropy}\,(d_1) + \frac{6}{14} entropy(d_2)
$$

The entropy of other features can be calculated in that way. When we have a feature with $k$ values, we will calculate $k$ different entropies (for $k$ disjoint datasets) and then take their weighted average.



### Cell 5 markdown
### **8.2.4 The Most Valuable Question<font color='#008DFF'> &#x2B24; </font>**

In order to determine the best question to ask first, we calculate the entropies of all features. The feature with the **smallest entropy** is the one that gives us the highest information gain.

In general, the **information gain** at a given node of a decision tree is taken to be the difference between the entropy at the node, and some combination of the entropies at the subtrees &mdash; typically the weighted average, but other ways can be devised.

### Cell 0 markdown
# **Module 9 &mdash; Hyperparameter Tuning and Validation**

### Cell 1 markdown
## **9.1  Validation Sets<font color='#FFCC00'>  &#x2B24; </font>**

We have already discussed that we commonly split the dataset $X$ into training and test datasets $X_{\mathit{train}}$ and $X_{\mathit{test}}$. We train a model on $X_{\mathit{train}}$, and then use $X_{\mathit{test}}$ to get an estimate of how the model will perform on new unseen points. In that sense, $X_{\mathit{test}}$ can help us counteract overfitting on $X_{\mathit{train}}$.


But even *before* we train the model, we have to decide what type of algorithm to use and then set a number of hyperparameters. Consider as a simple example the $k$-NN algorithm, where we must select a value of $k$, and also the type of distance to use. Suppose we want to try $20$ different values for $k$ and two different types of distances. That means there are 40 different combinations of settings for the hyperparameters, and we want to determine which combination is best. One simple idea is to train $40$ different models on $X_{\mathit{train}}$, then check their accuracy on the test set, and finally select the model with the best performance.

However, if we do check all 40 combinations of settings, then due to the large number of experiments, there is a chance that the performance of the best model on the test set may be the result of some *random* overfitting to the test set. This type of overfitting is not the same as the training overfitting, which is expected and systematic &mdash; in checking 40 combinations of settings, the *model itself* is *selected* so as fit $X_{\mathit{train}}$. However, such random overfitting can happen, and *if* it happens, our estimate of the model's real performance will not be accurate. So, if we 'deploy' such a model in a product, the product will turn out to have a subpar performance.

For that reason, we often split the set $X$ into three sets: $X_{\mathit{train}}$, $X_{\mathit{valid}}$ and $X_{\mathit{test}}$. The validation set $X_{\mathit{valid}}$ is used exactly like a test set in the above discussion: ***We use validation to select the best setting of the hyperparameters.***







### Cell 2 markdown
In practice, training accuracy/score is usually higher than both validation and test scores due to overfitting. But we also sometimes see that the validation score will be higher than the test score, which means that we do get some accidental overfitting on the validation set. However the gap between validation and test scores is usually relatively small, and the test score usually provides a reasonable estimate of the real performance of the model.

In general, when we select among a number of models, we should always pick the one with the highest validation score. And it is good practice to ***avoid looking at the test set until the very end of the model design process***.

### Cell 3 markdown
## **9.2 Validation for Picking the Best Model over Multiple Epochs<font color='#FFCC00'> &#x2B24; </font>**

![Loss and Accuracy convergence](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1D2UGcbn_hAPLWceUeLk24iqgtdhaagRh)

A common way of using validation sets is to detect overfitting *during* the training of more complex models that we will discuss in later chapters.

We often see the training loss decreasing over many epochs, but the validation loss (the value of the loss function on $X_{\mathit{valid}}$) stops decreasing, and may start to increase. Conversely, the training accuracy may keep increasing, but validation accuracy may not follow. So, another use of the validation set is to detect the epoch where the validation score reached its higher value (or began to decline), and use the model from that epoch.

### Cell 4 markdown
## **9.3  *k*-Fold Validation<font color='#008DFF'>  &#x2B24; </font>**

The use of a validation set means that we will have fewer points with which to train and test. Of course training data points are very valuable, especially in applications with small datasets. In order to avoid such 'waste' of training data, but also to get a possibly more reliable estimate for real performance, we use a process known as $k$-fold validation.

![k-fold validation](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1D43IkgGwLBl-oBNNLSstuRMqb8qZv4wE)



### Cell 5 markdown
In $k$-fold validation (where $k$ is a hyperparameter usually set to small values like 3, 5 or 10), we have a single training set $X_{\mathit{train}}$ that we split into $k$ 'folds', $X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}$. We then train $k$ different models where for the $i^{th}$ model, we use as a **validation** set fold $X_i$ and as a **training** set all folds except $X_i$. Then for each of these $k$ models we get a different validation score $v_i$, and we return as an overall validation score the average, $\frac{1}{k}\sum_{j=1}^k v_i$ .

One drawback of $k$-fold validation is that it requires more training time, as $k$ different models must be trained.




### Cell 6 markdown
## **9.4  Grid Search Notebook<font color='#369870'> &#x2B24; </font>**

Scikit-learn provides functionality that automates model selection after hyperparameter tuning. The 'grid search' approach trains and ($k$-fold) validates one model for each hyperparameter setting in a list of settings provided by the user. It then returns the model with the highest validation score.

### Cell 0 markdown
# **Module 10 &mdash; Attribute Selection**

### Cell 1 markdown
## **10.1 Selecting Meaningful Attributes<font color='#FFCC00'>  &#x2B24; </font>**
    
Consider the example of stock market data. There are millions of data points one can use as attributes. For the sake of argument, let's say there are $10^6$ attributes that are potentially available to us. We can imagine that within these $10^6$ attributes, there exist $10$ which, if selected, make the data points linearly separable. In that case, we could use a perceptron to completely predict days when the market is up or down, and try to become rich.

This is of course a fictional example, but not totally crazy. Many hedge funds and finance companies essentially work on detecting such 'market inefficiencies', where a small set of attributes expose an opportunity to make money.

Going back to our fictional example, we could attempt to find those 10 ideal attributes, by trying all possibilities. That is, for each subset of 10 attributes, we could restrict our data to those 10 attributes only, and then try to fit a perceptron. And we could keep trying until we found those 10 "golden" attributes.  But if we have $10^6$ attributes, that would require checking roughly $\left(10^6\right)^{10} = 10^{60}$ possibilities, which is an extremely large number (much more than the total number of atoms making up planet Earth!).

While we can use our human intuition and understanding in trying to find some good attributes, we would also like to have some automated way (that is, an algorithm) for doing that.

Selecting a small number of important features can also help with computational efficiency. Fewer features require less computation from smaller models, and therefore can more easily deployed in smaller devices.

We have already seen that $l_1$-[regularization](https://colab.research.google.com/drive/1EUF7z7f-pqqFEyhoxjbqGMX2H7f5eE6i?usp=drive_fs#scrollTo=s5oB2gT7vJor) can be used to select attributes. We will now describe some additional algorithms.






### Cell 2 markdown
## **10.2  Important Attributes via Random Forests<font color='#008DFF'>  &#x2B24; </font>**

When training a single decision tree, each attribute $i$ is used one or more times to split the dataset or its subsets. Each time attribute $i$ is used for a branching decision, we can measure the information gain for that decision. We can then compute a weighted information gain contributed by $i$ over the entire decision tree.

In a random forest we will train $N$ different decision trees, and thus we can compute an information gain $g_{i,j}$ for attribute $i$ in each decision tree $j$. Then, the overall importance for attribute $i$ can be computed as follows:

$$
    G_i = \sum_{j=1}^N g_{i,j}\, .
$$

Finally we can compute a normalized importance
$$
    \hat{G}_i = G_i\bigg/\sum_i G_i\, .
$$

The computation of feature importances adds very little extra computation to the training of the random forest, and as such they can be computed efficiently.

On the other hand, possible issues include:

* **Correlated features**. Datasets very often contain groups of highly correlated features. While the importance of a single feature may be high in isolation (i.e. in the absence of other features), that feature may end up getting assigned a lower importance when considered together with other features correlated with it. We can think of this intuitively as a dilution of the importance score through a sharing of 'credit' among the correlated features.
* **High Cardinality Features**. Features with many possible categorical values tend to receive higher importance, simply due to their higher ability to partition the dataset.


### Cell 3 markdown
## **10.3  Random Forests Notebook<font color='#369870'> &#x2B24; </font>**


Here is the [code notebook](https://colab.research.google.com/drive/1BUIOWXImNFzVJYs8gPnq2s8A6TXAaz3G?usp=drive_fs).

### Cell 4 markdown
## **10.4  Greedy Attribute Elimination<font color='#008DFF'> &#x2B24; </font>**

Suppose we have a dataset $X$ with $d$ features. We can ask the question: if we had to remove only one feature, what would that be? Which choice would be safest?

One natural way of answering the question is to train $d$ different models $m_{1},\ldots,m_d$, where $m_i$ is trained on the data $X$, but with feature $i$ **dropped** from $X$. Then, if $m_j$ is the model with *highest* reference score (e.g. accuracy) among the $d$ models, that would imply that attribute $j$ is not so important.

After removing attribute from $j$, we get a new dataset $X'$, and the process can continue in exactly the same way with $X'$.

By doing this a number of $k$ times, we will have eliminated $k$ attributes. Because in each iteration we 'greedily' pick the feature that currently appears to be not so important, the whole process is known as greedy attribute elimination.

This process can be applied with any model (not just random forests), but one problem with it is that it requires the training of many models. Eliminating $k$ attributes means that we would need to train $O(dk)$ models.  


### Cell 5 markdown
## **10.5  Greedy Feature Elimination Notebook<font color='#369870'> &#x2B24; </font>**


Here is the [code notebook](https://colab.research.google.com/drive/1BToc2S_mbMHCYIzOdYnIj7iitBNibU1U?usp=drive_fs).

### Cell 6 markdown
## **10.6. Permutation-Based Feature Importance<font color='#008DFF'> &#x2B24; </font>**

Permutation-based feature selection is a method that is not prone to the issues of random forests. It requires less computation than greedy elimination because it trains only **one** model $m$, and after $k$ different rounds of validation (on different validation sets), outputs an importance measure for each attribute.

The main idea is that in the $i^{th}$ round of validation, we take the validation set $X_{\mathit{valid}}$, *shuffle* its $i^{th}$ column and then report the validation score of model $m$ on it. For higher robustness, we can repeat the same procedures with other random shuffles and report an average validation.

In general, attributes whose permutation results in lower validation, are considered more important.


**Note:** Permuting a coordinate does not affect certain general statistics for the attribute (such as mean, variance, and other moments). Unlike greedy elimination, shuffling a column allows reuse of the same learning model.

### Cell 7 markdown
## **10.7 Permutation-Based Feature Importance Pseudocode<font color='#008DFF'> &#x2B24; </font>**

 * Inputs: fitted predictive model $m$, tabular dataset (training or validation) $D$.
 * Compute the reference score $s$ of the model $m$ on data $D$ (for example, the accuracy for a classifier or $R^2$ for a regressor).
 * For each feature $j$ (column of $D)$:
   - For each repetition $k$ in $1,\ldots,K$:
     - Randomly shuffle column $j$ of dataset $D$ to generate a corrupted version of the data, $\tilde{D}_{k,j}$.
     - Compute the score $s_{k,j}$ of model $m$ on corrupted data $\tilde{D}_{k,j}$.
   - Compute importance $i_j$ for feature $f_j$, defined as
   $$
   i_j=s-\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^K s_{k,j} \, .
   $$


```

The intuition is that such a function $f$ can then used to give predictions for the numerical labels of unseen points.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Modeling the data.** Data often contains some natural variability, possibly due to measurement noise etc. The given data appear to approximately follow a line with some added variability. So, we can use our intuition and *model* the data as a line; that is, we assume that the function is the form of a line:\\r\\n$$\\r\\n   y = ax+b\\\\, .\\r\\n$$\\r\\n\\r\\nHere $a,b$ are unknown parameters. The equation $y = ax+b$ is thus a set of infinitely many *hypotheses*, or *models* for the 'law' that generates data, and we want to find values for $a$ and $b$ that give the best possible hypothesis or *model* - that is, the model that gives the best approximations $f(X_i)$ for $y_i$.\\r\\n\\r\\nData modeling has its origins in Statistics. Indeed, Machine Learning is historically rooted in Statistics. The material of this section predates Machine Learning by many decades, or even centuries. But it is in the core of nearly all algorithms in current ML and Deep Learning.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 2 markdown\\r\\n## **3.2 Loss Function<font color='#FF5733'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose we have now fixed a model $f$. It is not too difficult to quantify the error of approximation on a single data point $X_i$:\\r\\n$$\\r\\n    e_i = |f(X_i) - y_i|\\r\\n$$\\r\\n\\r\\nWe can extend these individual errors to a global error, that we will call the *loss function*, defined as follows:\\r\\n$$\\r\\n    L = \\\\frac{1}{n} \\\\cdot\\\\sum_{i=1}^n e_i^2\\r\\n$$\\r\\n\\r\\nWith this definition, $L$ is often called the ***mean squared error***. When $L$ is small we expect that most individual errors $e_i$ will also be small. So, it makes sense to find a model $f(x) = ax + b$ that yields a small loss $L$. Recall that $X$ and $y$ are given to us, so $L$ only depends on the choice of $a,b$. We will sometimes write $L(a,b)$ to emphasize that.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 3 markdown\\r\\n### **3.2.1 Why MSE?**\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are potentially many ways to define a global loss. For example, we could have picked the much simpler function $L = (1/n) \\\\sum_i e_i$, or even\\r\\n$L = max_i\\\\{e_i\\\\}$. MSE is a popular choice, though, mainly due to its nice mathematical properties.\\r\\n\\r\\n~![Convex function illustration](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1zfYT544LpWnnYN-IDPB1zAVXMDuHVTq3)\\r\\n\\r\\nThis 3D plot demonstrates these properties. Here the two axes correspond to the variables $a,b$ and the third axis corresponds to $L(a,b)$. Its characteristic shape illustrates that the function is convex, and as such, it has a unique local and global minimum. In other words, there is a unique setting of $(a,b)$ that minimize the loss. A general way to think about the minimum is this: For each value $c>0$ there is a set of points $(a,b)$ that satisfy $L(a,b)=c$, and that set is called a function level set - in the above 3D plot all points in a level set get precisely the same color. The level set for $c=0$ is a unique point.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe function is also smooth, or 'differentiable' at any point $(a,b)$. And that brings us to calculus.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 4 markdown\\r\\n## **3.3 Gradient Descent in a Nutshell<font color='#FF5733'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 5 markdown\\r\\n### **3.3.1 Gradient Descent in One Variable**\\r\\n\\r\\nConsider a simple function with one variable $y=x^2$ (by the way, these $x,y$ have nothing to do with our model $y=ax+b$, we are just reusing sumbols).\\r\\n\\r\\n![Quadratic Function](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1rSz4bISQVNgtHGhFQyJnh8_pk0j39P66)\\r\\n\\r\\nYou may recall from calculus that the derivative of this function is $f'(x) = 2x$. By its definition, the derivative at some point $x$ gives us the rate at which the function changes. Moreover, the sign of the derivative tells us whether the function is locally increasing. As an example, at point $x=2$ we get a positive derivative of 4, which means that the function is increasing as $x$ increases. On the other hand, the derivative at $x=-2$ is equal to $-4$, which means that the function is decreasing.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf we then want to ***nudge*** our point $x$ towards the minimum of the function we can do an update of the following form:\\r\\n$$\\r\\n     x \\\\leftarrow x - \\\\rho \\\\cdot \\\\mathit{sign}\\\\,(f'(x))\\\\, ,\\r\\n$$\\r\\nwhere $\\\\rho$ is a step size. We can carefully check and see that independent of whether the derivative has a positive or negative sign, if $\\\\rho$ is sufficiently small, the updated $x$ will be closer to the minimum of the function.\\r\\n\\r\\nA good $\\\\rho$ should depend on how fast the function changes, i.e. in the derivative itself. It is then more natural to make $\\\\rho$ independent of the derivative and use the following update rule:\\r\\n$$\\r\\n     x \\\\leftarrow x - \\\\rho \\\\cdot f'(x)\\\\, .\\r\\n$$\\r\\n\\r\\nThis update steps can be *repeated* multiple times to bring our point $x$ closer to the minimum. Because these iterative updates nudge our point to descend towards the minimum, and we use the derivative (the gradient for one dimension), we call this update process ***gradient descent***.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Exercise:** Think about the effect of picking a step size $\\\\rho<1$, or $\\\\rho>1$, or $\\\\rho=1$.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 6 markdown\\r\\n### **3.3.2 Partial Derivatives**\\r\\n\\r\\nConsider now a 2-variate function, e.g. $f(a,b) = a^2+3ab+b^2+3$.\\r\\nOne can use partial derivatives to get information about the speed of the function in each coordinate/axis separately.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor example, we can consider $b$ as a fixed number, and compute the partial derivative of $f$ with respect to $a$:\\r\\n\\r\\n$$\\r\\n    \\\\frac{\\\\partial f }{\\\\partial a} = 2a + 3b \\\\, .\\r\\n$$\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 7 markdown\\r\\n### **3.3.3 Gradient Descent in Multiple Dimensions**\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose now we have a fixed point $(a,b)$ and we want to nudge it towards a minimum of the function $f(x,y)$. This can done by finding the update rule along the $x$-axis only, as if $b$ was fixed, and vice-versa. This gives us two\\r\\ndifferent single-variable update rules:\\r\\n\\r\\n$$\\r\\na \\\\leftarrow a - \\\\rho\\\\,\\\\frac{\\\\partial f}{\\\\partial a}\\\\, ,\\r\\n### Cell 0 markdown\\r\\n# **Module 6 &mdash; Regularization**\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 1 markdown\\r\\n## **6.1 Sensitivity to Noise<font color='#FFCC00'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\nConsider as a visual example the following 2D dataset.\\r\\n\\r\\n![2D dataset with 3 classes](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=17Wdo7zfVOBjL5qLtkmpZBRKPfmzAewDH)\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose now we have one point $x=(x_1,x_2)$, and a second one $x' = (x_1+\\\\epsilon,x_2)$, which comes from adding a bit of noise $\\\\epsilon$ to $x_1$.\\r\\nSince we expect that nearby points in space should belong to the same class, the classifier should not be too sensitive to the noise.\\r\\n\\r\\nLet's then consider the net input of a classifier with weights $[w_0,w_1,w_2]$ for our two points:\\r\\n\\r\\n* $z = w_1 x_1 + w_2 x_2 + w_0$, &nbsp; and\\r\\n* $z' \\\\: = \\\\: w_1(x_1+\\\\epsilon) + w_2x_2 + w_0 \\\\: = \\\\: z + w_1\\\\epsilon$.\\r\\n\\r\\nIntuitively, if $w_1$ is large, then even a small amount of noise $\\\\epsilon$  will cause a large change in the net input $z$.\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom this we can see that smaller values of the parameters $w_i$ would be preferred, since they may lead to better generalization. That leads us to regularization, a class of techniques meant to fight overfitting.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 2 markdown\\r\\n## **6.2 Regularization<font color='#008DFF'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\nWe would like to impose a penalty to large values for the parameters $w$. This can be done by adding a penalty to the loss function. More specifically, if our loss function is $L$, we can then define a new loss function\\r\\n$$\\r\\n    L_{\\\\mathit{reg}}(w) = L(w) + \\\\lambda\\\\,\\\\|w\\\\|_2^2 \\\\, ,\\r\\n$$\\r\\nwhere $\\\\lambda$ is some hyperparameter that controls the strength of regularization.\\r\\nThis new loss function imposes a penalty to having a large 2-norm of the weights, which is the reason it is called $l_2$-regularization. Note that if $\\\\lambda$ is large, the optimization favors those choices of $w$ for which $\\\\|w\\\\|_2$ is smaller, which in turn forces smaller values for the individual weights $w_i$. Also note that some applications may use the unsquared 2-norm,\\r\\n$$\\r\\n    L_{\\\\mathit{reg}}(w) = L(w) + \\\\lambda\\\\,\\\\|w\\\\|_2 \\\\, ,\\r\\n$$\\r\\nwhich penalizes the growth in vector norm linearly rather than quadratically. (With the learning algorithms covered in this course, the squared 2-norm is more commonly used.)\\r\\n\\r\\nAlternatively, we can perform $l_1$-regularization by defining this loss function as\\r\\n$$\\r\\n    L_{\\\\mathit{reg}}(w) = L(w) + \\\\lambda\\\\,\\\\|w\\\\|_1 \\\\, .\\r\\n$$\\r\\n\\r\\nRegularization can also be applied to linear regression, where $l_2$ and $l_1$ regularization are known as *Ridge* and *Lasso* regression, respectively. A mix of $l_2$ and $l_1$ regularization that adds two different regularization terms is known as *elastic nets*.\\r\\n\\r\\nThese new loss functions penalize for large weights and intuitively will make it harder for the line to be 'dragged' by one outlier; hence, the model will be less prone to overfitting. These loss functions can be optimized with an appropriate optimizer. Usually a standard optimizer can treat  (via gradient descent) the $l_2$ regularization without any difficulties, but optimizing with $l_1$-regularization is a harder computational problem.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 3 markdown\\r\\n## **6.3 Geometric Understanding of Regularization<font color='#FFCC00'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n![Landscape of a Convex Function](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1ERFb77r-ogUpDrAddLok5VoJ2t0cKlL9)\\r\\n\\r\\nConsider the pictured landscape of a loss function of a two dimensional model, where the $z$-axis is the value of the loss. Pairs $(x,y)$ that give the same loss value $L$ are plotted with the same color at height $z=L$. These contours are called a **level set** of the function. Level sets form closed curves if we 'project' them onto the 2D plane.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 4 markdown\\r\\nLet's now consider the $l_2$-regularization loss function $L_{\\\\mathit{reg}}(w) = L(w) + \\\\lambda\\\\,\\\\|w\\\\|_2^2$. This is a sum of two different functions: a loss (or 'cost') $L(w)$, and a penalty $\\\\lambda\\\\,\\\\|w\\\\|_2$. The following image shows the contour plots of both functions in a simple 2D example. The contour plot of the regularization part consists of circles, because the set of points that are on the same level $L$ define the circle $w_1^2+w_2^2=L$.\\r\\n\\r\\n![Contour plots for l2-regularization](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1ERqBeXKfJ0m56rdqTzR0gGnH1MicNZoj)\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are two different points that minimize each part separately, but their sum is minimized at a different intermediate point. In fact, in the case of $l_2$-regression, there is a unique tangent line to the corresponding contour curves of the two functions.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 5 markdown\\r\\nNow consider the $l_1$-loss regularization $L_{reg}(w) = L(w) + \\\\lambda\\\\,\\\\|w\\\\|_1$\\r\\nand the corresponding contour plots.\\r\\n\\r\\n<img src='https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1ESQOBvxxjtolKx0qtLVLMHts0kMjfMS1' width=400 height=320>\\r\\n\\r\\nIn this case, the contour plots of the regularization term are squares around the origin, since the equations $|w_1|+|w_2|=L$ define squares.  Again, the sum of the two functions is minimized at an intermediate point.\\r\\n\\r\\n$l_1$-regularization has a very interesting characteristic. Because the loss function $L(w)$ is convex, and $\\\\|w\\\\|_1$ has square-like contours, it is very likely that the corners of $\\\\|w\\\\|_1$ will penetrate deeper towards the center of the convex contours of the loss function. Intuitively, the sum of the two functions will be minimized at these corners of $\\\\|w\\\\|_1$ (even in higher dimensions), i.e. at poi…1654 tokens truncated…raining and test datasets $X_{\\\\mathit{train}}$ and $X_{\\\\mathit{test}}$. We train a model on $X_{\\\\mathit{train}}$, and then use $X_{\\\\mathit{test}}$ to get an estimate of how the model will perform on new unseen points. In that sense, $X_{\\\\mathit{test}}$ can help us counteract overfitting on $X_{\\\\mathit{train}}$.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nBut even *before* we train the model, we have to decide what type of algorithm to use and then set a number of hyperparameters. Consider as a simple example the $k$-NN algorithm, where we must select a value of $k$, and also the type of distance to use. Suppose we want to try $20$ different values for $k$ and two different types of distances. That means there are 40 different combinations of settings for the hyperparameters, and we want to determine which combination is best. One simple idea is to train $40$ different models on $X_{\\\\mathit{train}}$, then check their accuracy on the test set, and finally select the model with the best performance.\\r\\n\\r\\nHowever, if we do check all 40 combinations of settings, then due to the large number of experiments, there is a chance that the performance of the best model on the test set may be the result of some *random* overfitting to the test set. This type of overfitting is not the same as the training overfitting, which is expected and systematic &mdash; in checking 40 combinations of settings, the *model itself* is *selected* so as fit $X_{\\\\mathit{train}}$. However, such random overfitting can happen, and *if* it happens, our estimate of the model's real performance will not be accurate. So, if we 'deploy' such a model in a product, the product will turn out to have a subpar performance.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor that reason, we often split the set $X$ into three sets: $X_{\\\\mathit{train}}$, $X_{\\\\mathit{valid}}$ and $X_{\\\\mathit{test}}$. The validation set $X_{\\\\mathit{valid}}$ is used exactly like a test set in the above discussion: ***We use validation to select the best setting of the hyperparameters.***\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 2 markdown\\r\\nIn practice, training accuracy/score is usually higher than both validation and test scores due to overfitting. But we also sometimes see that the validation score will be higher than the test score, which means that we do get some accidental overfitting on the validation set. However the gap between validation and test scores is usually relatively small, and the test score usually provides a reasonable estimate of the real performance of the model.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn general, when we select among a number of models, we should always pick the one with the highest validation score. And it is good practice to ***avoid looking at the test set until the very end of the model design process***.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 3 markdown\\r\\n## **9.2 Validation for Picking the Best Model over Multiple Epochs<font color='#FFCC00'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\n![Loss and Accuracy convergence](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1D2UGcbn_hAPLWceUeLk24iqgtdhaagRh)\\r\\n\\r\\nA common way of using validation sets is to detect overfitting *during* the training of more complex models that we will discuss in later chapters.\\r\\n\\r\\nWe often see the training loss decreasing over many epochs, but the validation loss (the value of the loss function on $X_{\\\\mathit{valid}}$) stops decreasing, and may start to increase. Conversely, the training accuracy may keep increasing, but validation accuracy may not follow. So, another use of the validation set is to detect the epoch where the validation score reached its higher value (or began to decline), and use the model from that epoch.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 4 markdown\\r\\n## **9.3  *k*-Fold Validation<font color='#008DFF'>  &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\nThe use of a validation set means that we will have fewer points with which to train and test. Of course training data points are very valuable, especially in applications with small datasets. In order to avoid such 'waste' of training data, but also to get a possibly more reliable estimate for real performance, we use a process known as $k$-fold validation.\\r\\n\\r\\n![k-fold validation](https://docs.google.com/uc?export=download&id=1D43IkgGwLBl-oBNNLSstuRMqb8qZv4wE)\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 5 markdown\\r\\nIn $k$-fold validation (where $k$ is a hyperparameter usually set to small values like 3, 5 or 10), we have a single training set $X_{\\\\mathit{train}}$ that we split into $k$ 'folds', $X_{1},\\\\ldots,X_{k}$. We then train $k$ different models where for the $i^{th}$ model, we use as a **validation** set fold $X_i$ and as a **training** set all folds except $X_i$. Then for each of these $k$ models we get a different validation score $v_i$, and we return as an overall validation score the average, $\\\\frac{1}{k}\\\\sum_{j=1}^k v_i$ .\\r\\n\\r\\nOne drawback of $k$-fold validation is that it requires more training time, as $k$ different models must be trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 6 markdown\\r\\n## **9.4  Grid Search Notebook<font color='#369870'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\nScikit-learn provides functionality that automates model selection after hyperparameter tuning. The 'grid search' approach trains and ($k$-fold) validates one model for each hyperparameter setting in a list of settings provided by the user. It then returns the model with the highest validation score.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nHere is the [code notebook](https://colab.research.google.com/drive/1EYK3YTYoNd6p3pOrXxMuqod2cR6TJm64?usp=drive_fs).\\r\\n### Cell 0 markdown\\r\\n# **Module 10 &mdash; Attribute Selection**\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 1 markdown\\r\\n## **10.1 Selecting Meaningful Attributes<font color='#FFCC00'>  &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n    \\r\\nConsider the example of stock market data. There are millions of data points one can use as attributes. For the sake of argument, let's say there are $10^6$ attributes that are potentially available to us. We can imagine that within these $10^6$ attributes, there exist $10$ which, if selected, make the data points linearly separable. In that case, we could use a perceptron to completely predict days when the market is up or down, and try to become rich.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is of course a fictional example, but not totally crazy. Many hedge funds and finance companies essentially work on detecting such 'market inefficiencies', where a small set of attributes expose an opportunity to make money.\\r\\n\\r\\nGoing back to our fictional example, we could attempt to find those 10 ideal attributes, by trying all possibilities. That is, for each subset of 10 attributes, we could restrict our data to those 10 attributes only, and then try to fit a perceptron. And we could keep trying until we found those 10 \\\"golden\\\" attributes.  But if we have $10^6$ attributes, that would require checking roughly $\\\\left(10^6\\\\right)^{10} = 10^{60}$ possibilities, which is an extremely large number (much more than the total number of atoms making up planet Earth!).\\r\\n\\r\\nWhile we can use our human intuition and understanding in trying to find some good attributes, we would also like to have some automated way (that is, an algorithm) for doing that.\\r\\n\\r\\nSelecting a small number of important features can also help with computational efficiency. Fewer features require less computation from smaller models, and therefore can more easily deployed in smaller devices.\\r\\n\\r\\nWe have already seen that $l_1$-[regularization](https://colab.research.google.com/drive/1EUF7z7f-pqqFEyhoxjbqGMX2H7f5eE6i?usp=drive_fs#scrollTo=s5oB2gT7vJor) can be used to select attributes. We will now describe some additional algorithms.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 2 markdown\\r\\n## **10.2  Important Attributes via Random Forests<font color='#008DFF'>  &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen training a single decision tree, each attribute $i$ is used one or more times to split the dataset or its subsets. Each time attribute $i$ is used for a branching decision, we can measure the information gain for that decision. We can then compute a weighted information gain contributed by $i$ over the entire decision tree.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn a random forest we will train $N$ different decision trees, and thus we can compute an information gain $g_{i,j}$ for attribute $i$ in each decision tree $j$. Then, the overall importance for attribute $i$ can be computed as follows:\\r\\n\\r\\n$$\\r\\n    G_i = \\\\sum_{j=1}^N g_{i,j}\\\\, .\\r\\n$$\\r\\n\\r\\nFinally we can compute a normalized importance\\r\\n$$\\r\\n    \\\\hat{G}_i = G_i\\\\bigg/\\\\sum_i G_i\\\\, .\\r\\n$$\\r\\n\\r\\nThe computation of feature importances adds very little extra computation to the training of the random forest, and as such they can be computed efficiently.\\r\\n\\r\\nOn the other hand, possible issues include:\\r\\n\\r\\n* **Correlated features**. Datasets very often contain groups of highly correlated features. While the importance of a single feature may be high in isolation (i.e. in the absence of other features), that feature may end up getting assigned a lower importance when considered together with other features correlated with it. We can think of this intuitively as a dilution of the importance score through a sharing of 'credit' among the correlated features.\\r\\n* **High Cardinality Features**. Features with many possible categorical values tend to receive higher importance, simply due to their higher ability to partition the dataset.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 3 markdown\\r\\n## **10.3  Random Forests Notebook<font color='#369870'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nHere is the [code notebook](https://colab.research.google.com/drive/1BUIOWXImNFzVJYs8gPnq2s8A6TXAaz3G?usp=drive_fs).\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 4 markdown\\r\\n## **10.4  Greedy Attribute Elimination<font color='#008DFF'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose we have a dataset $X$ with $d$ features. We can ask the question: if we had to remove only one feature, what would that be? Which choice would be safest?\\r\\n\\r\\nOne natural way of answering the question is to train $d$ different models $m_{1},\\\\ldots,m_d$, where $m_i$ is trained on the data $X$, but with feature $i$ **dropped** from $X$. Then, if $m_j$ is the model with *highest* reference score (e.g. accuracy) among the $d$ models, that would imply that attribute $j$ is not so important.\\r\\n\\r\\nAfter removing attribute from $j$, we get a new dataset $X'$, and the process can continue in exactly the same way with $X'$.\\r\\n\\r\\nBy doing this a number of $k$ times, we will have eliminated $k$ attributes. Because in each iteration we 'greedily' pick the feature that currently appears to be not so important, the whole process is known as greedy attribute elimination.\\r\\n\\r\\nThis process can be applied with any model (not just random forests), but one problem with it is that it requires the training of many models. Eliminating $k$ attributes means that we would need to train $O(dk)$ models.  \\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 5 markdown\\r\\n## **10.5  Greedy Feature Elimination Notebook<font color='#369870'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nHere is the [code notebook](https://colab.research.google.com/drive/1BToc2S_mbMHCYIzOdYnIj7iitBNibU1U?usp=drive_fs).\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 6 markdown\\r\\n## **10.6. Permutation-Based Feature Importance<font color='#008DFF'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\nPermutation-based feature selection is a method that is not prone to the issues of random forests. It requires less computation than greedy elimination because it trains only **one** model $m$, and after $k$ different rounds of validation (on different validation sets), outputs an importance measure for each attribute.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe main idea is that in the $i^{th}$ round of validation, we take the validation set $X_{\\\\mathit{valid}}$, *shuffle* its $i^{th}$ column and then report the validation score of model $m$ on it. For higher robustness, we can repeat the same procedures with other random shuffles and report an average validation.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn general, attributes whose permutation results in lower validation, are considered more important.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n**Note:** Permuting a coordinate does not affect certain general statistics for the attribute (such as mean, variance, and other moments). Unlike greedy elimination, shuffling a column allows reuse of the same learning model.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 7 markdown\\r\\n## **10.7 Permutation-Based Feature Importance Pseudocode<font color='#008DFF'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\n * Inputs: fitted predictive model $m$, tabular dataset (training or validation) $D$.\\r\\n * Compute the reference score $s$ of the model $m$ on data $D$ (for example, the accuracy for a classifier or $R^2$ for a regressor).\\r\\n * For each feature $j$ (column of $D)$:\\r\\n   - For each repetition $k$ in $1,\\\\ldots,K$:\\r\\n     - Randomly shuffle column $j$ of dataset $D$ to generate a corrupted version of the data, $\\\\tilde{D}_{k,j}$.\\r\\n     - Compute the score $s_{k,j}$ of model $m$ on corrupted data $\\\\tilde{D}_{k,j}$.\\r\\n   - Compute importance $i_j$ for feature $f_j$, defined as\\r\\n   $$\\r\\n   i_j=s-\\\\frac{1}{K}\\\\sum_{k=1}^K s_{k,j} \\\\, .\\r\\n   $$\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\"}}"
  },
  {
    "type": "input_text",
    "text": "{\"i\":1,\"status\":\"fulfilled\",\"value\":{\"chunk_id\":\"f869f2\",\"wall_time_seconds\":0.2732015,\"exit_code\":1,\"original_token_count\":86,\"output\":\"At line:4 char:152\\r\\n+ ... |shuffle|feature|bootstrap' analysis/week7/{6_Regularization,8_Decisi ...\\r\\n+                                                                 ~\\r\\nMissing argument in parameter list.\\r\\n    + CategoryInfo          : ParserError: (:) [], ParentContainsErrorRecordException\\r\\n    + FullyQualifiedErrorId : MissingArgument\\r\\n \\r\\n\"}}"

analysis/week7/6_Regularization.txt:46:These new loss functions penalize for large weights and intuitively will make it harder for the line to be 'dragged' by one outlier; hence, the model will be less prone to overfitting. These loss functions can be optimized with an appropriate optimizer. analysis/week7/8_Decision_Trees.txt:132:## **8.5  Decision Trees for Regression<font color='#008DFF'>  &#x2B24; </font>**
analysis/week7/8_Decision_Trees.txt:152:where $\mathrm{E}[y]$ is taken to be the average of the numerical labels of the points in $D$. Intuitively, when the variance is small, all numerical labels $y$ for our dataset $D$ must fall in a very small range around $\mathrm{E}[y]$, and thus a very simple yet effective regression model for $D$ would simply output the prediction $\hat{y} = \mathrm{E}[y]$ for every new point $x$. When the prediction is $\hat{y} = \mathrm{E}[y]$, the variance $\mathrm{Var}(D)$ may be viewed as the mean squared error.
analysis/week7/8_Decision_Trees.txt:156:As in categorical-output decision trees, the recursion can stop when a given subdataset $D'=(X',y')$ has its variance $\mathrm{Var}(D')$ below some threshold, in which case the prediction will be equal to $\mathrm{E}[y']$. Of course, alternative measures for the 'variance gain' can also be used, which accounts for one of the hyperparameters of the *scikit-learn* [decision tree regressor](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.tree.DecisionTreeRegressor.html#sklearn.tree.DecisionTreeRegressor) .
analysis/week7/11_Ensembles_and_Boosting.txt:42:## **11.3  Bootstrapped Aggregation (Bagging)<font color='#FFCC00'> &#x2B24; </font>**
analysis/week7/11_Ensembles_and_Boosting.txt:47:This observation underlies the intuition of a method of ensemble learning known as *bagging*, short for '*Bootstrapped AGgregation*'.
analysis/week7/11_Ensembles_and_Boosting.txt:146:The basic idea behind gradient boosting is better understood in the context of regression. Suppose we have $n$ points and their numerical labels $(X_i,y_i)$ and we have learned some regression model $h_0$ that makes a prediction
analysis/week7/11_Ensembles_and_Boosting.txt:153:We can then try to add another model $h_1$ that will learn the residuals, i.e. design a regression model for the points $X_i$, with numerical labels $r_i$. The motivation is this: *if* we are able to learn a model $h_1$ such that $r_i = h_1(X_i)$, then we can completely correct the original model $h_0$, by using the 'composite' ensemble model:
analysis/week7/11_Ensembles_and_Boosting.txt:159:Of course, $h_1$ is not expected to be a fully perfect model for the regression problem $(X,r_i)$, so $E(x)$ will still have some residual error that we can try to model with yet another model $h_2$, and the process can continue in this way for a number of rounds $T$.

```text
Sí. Veo dos alternativas que podemos desarrollar con los modelos del curso:

- **Aprender la corrección de la persistencia:** predecir el cambio de presión a +15 minutos y sumarlo a la presión actual. Así el modelo se concentra en cuándo y cuánto cambia n1. Esta idea ya aparecía en la versión extensa; podemos convertirla en el eje de una propuesta mucho más sencilla.
- **Predecir según el estado del tanque:** comparar un modelo único con modelos separados para llenado, vaciado y nivel casi estable. El estado se determinaría únicamente con cambios de nivel pasados.

En los casos principales revisados, ChebNet, SAM-kNN y AGN predicen niveles de presión/carga; los residuos de diagnóstico de fugas cumplen otra función. Ninguna de estas alternativas permite afirmar por sí sola una novedad mundial. Nuestro aporte tendría que demostrarse mediante una comparación controlada en n1, con las seis señales seleccionadas.

**Note 3:** In the update formula for the weights, $b_t(X_i)$ is the label predicted for point $X_i$, and so $y_i b_t(X_i)$ can be either $1$ when the true and predicted labels are the same, or $-1$ otherwise. If classifier $b_t$ has a positive importance weight $a_t$, then when the true and predicted weights are different, the weight of point $i$ increases, and more attention will be given to it in the training of future classifiers. Otherwise, the weight decreases, and less attention will be given to it in future training. For classifiers that are "untrustworthy" ($a_t<0$), the effect on the importance weight of $X_i$ is *reversed*.

**Note 4:** AdaBoost requires that classifiers be trained sequentially, i.e. one at a time. However, when the ensemble is deployed, these classifiers can process the input independently, before their predictions are combined in the final voting step.


### Cell 10 markdown
## **11.6 Gradient Boosting<font color='#FFCC00'> &#x2B24; </font>**

Gradient Boosting is a more recent method for constructing ensembles of classifiers. One of its implementations ([xgboost](https://xgboost.readthedocs.io/en/stable/)) has been widely used in Kaggle competitions, mostly by using [boosted trees](https://xgboost.readthedocs.io/en/stable/tutorials/model.html).

The basic idea behind gradient boosting is better understood in the context of regression. Suppose we have $n$ points and their numerical labels $(X_i,y_i)$ and we have learned some regression model $h_0$ that makes a prediction

$$
     \hat{y}_i = h_0(X_i) \, .
$$

Now, for every point $X_i$ we can calculate the residual error $r_i = y_i - \hat{y}_i$. If the first model can be improved, then it is likely the case that there will be some type of 'pattern' in these residuals.
We can then try to add another model $h_1$ that will learn the residuals, i.e. design a regression model for the points $X_i$, with numerical labels $r_i$. The motivation is this: *if* we are able to learn a model $h_1$ such that $r_i = h_1(X_i)$, then we can completely correct the original model $h_0$, by using the 'composite' ensemble model:

$$
    E(x) = h_0(x) + h_1(x) \, .
$$

Of course, $h_1$ is not expected to be a fully perfect model for the regression problem $(X,r_i)$, so $E(x)$ will still have some residual error that we can try to model with yet another model $h_2$, and the process can continue in this way for a number of rounds $T$.

Results\n\n### 3.1. Data Description\n\nThis study employed the L-Town water distribution model from the BattLeDIM competition [44]. L-Town is a small hypothetical urban WDN consisting of 785 nodes, 909 pipes, one pump, and three valves. In addition, 33 pressure sensors are installed at different nodes within the L-Town network. The L-Town network is adopted as the evaluation case study owing to its established status as an authoritative benchmark from the BattLeDIM competition, which ensures the reproducibility and comparability of our findings against state-of-the-art methodologies. Furthermore, despite its moderate scale, the network features highly heterogeneous topological attributes and integrates critical operational components (such as the pump and valves) capable of replicating complex, realistic hydraulic dynamics. Crucially, the deployment of 33 pressure sensors against 785 nodes represents a highly sparse sensing configuration. This alignment with the practical budgetary and infrastructural constraints of real-world water utilities provides a rigorous environment to validate the robustness of the proposed leakage localization framework.\n\nFigure 4 illustrates the topology of the L-Town network and the locations of the pressure sensors. In the figure, pressure sensors are marked in red, while the green squares represent reservoirs. Figure 5 presents the L-Town network after the introduction of virtual nodes.\n\nImage\n\nFigure 4. L-Town network topology (red: pressure sensors; green squares: reservoirs).\n\nImage\n\nFigure 5. L-Town network with virtual nodes added.\n\nIn this study, the L-Town network was divided into seven regions. The number of partitions was determined with reference to the scale of the network and the number of monitoring points, aiming to ensure sufficient sample size within each region while balancing computational complexity and spatial resolution. Figure 6 presents the virtual node partitioning results of the L-Town network.\n\nImage\n\nFigure 6. Virtual node partitioning of the L-Town network (triangles: monitoring points).\n## 4. Discussion\n\nL-Town is a well-established and widely used benchmark network for leakage detection and localization in water distribution systems. Originally developed for the BattLeDIM competition, it has since been extensively adopted as a standardized testbed for evaluating and comparing different leakage detection and localization methods. For example, during the competition, the Cheng00 team employed change-point detection and statistical analysis for leakage detection and localization [50], the D and W team used pressure sensitivity analysis to identify potentially leaking pipes [51], and the Leakbusters team combined data-driven pressure reconstruction with hydraulic simulation [52]. Following the competition, a broader range of approaches have been investigated. Rajabi, et al. [53] proposed a method based on a Conditional Deep Convolutional Generative Adversarial Network (CDCGAN) and the Structural Similarity Index Measure (SSIM) for real-time leak detection and localization on L-Town, achieving approximately 70% detection accuracy, while Wang, et al. [54] developed a hydraulic-model-free approach based on graph theory and Bayesian reasoning, with localization errors below 300 m for all 10 leak points and below 70 m in some cases. Meanwhile, network partitioning has emerged as an important strategy for simplifying leakage localization. Dong, et al. [55] combined three-order embedding, K-means clustering, and Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) for network partitioning and leak localization, incorporating pressure sensitivity, spatial information, and network connectivity, while Pérez-Sandoval, et al. [56] integrated Spectral Clustering with Bidirectional Long Short-Term Memory (Bi-LSTM) and achieved 85% effectiveness across 117 unseen leakage scenarios on L-Town.\n\nWithin this research landscape, the present study focuses on partitioning-based leakage localization while further addressing the prediction stage through an interpretable Stacking ensemble framework. Rather than depending on a single learning architecture, the proposed approach integrates RF, MLP, and XGBoost to exploit complementary predictive characteristics, and further reduces the sensing requirement through an optimized subset of eight pressure sensors. On the L-Town benchmark, the framework achieves an overall localization accuracy of 0.9667 and a Macro-F1 score of 0.9666, demonstrating strong localization performance. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nDetection and localization of pipeline leakage of water distribution networks based on graph convolutional networks - Li - 2025 - Computer-Aided Civil and Infrastructure Engineering - Wiley Online Library (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/mice.70173)\nciteturn93search1 [wordlim: 200] Published: 10 months ago; Crawled: last week; Daniel et al. (2022) employed a two-stage framework combining sequential detection and model-based localization; a semi-supervised 1D-CNN autoencoder was used to reconstruct pressure signals for leak localization(Tornyeviadzi & Seidu, 2023); a graph-based state interpolation (GSI) was performed to complete hydraulic states and applied dictionary-learning (DL) classification, achieving localization from pressure series sampled at 5-min intervals(Irofti et al., 2022); Ma et al. (2022) combined SCADA-driven state estimation with real-time demand calibration and history–simulation differencing for leakage detection; Wan et al. Because the two study datasets employed here (the simulated WDN and L-Town) do not contain the high-resolution pressure series required by those methods, an effective head-to-head comparison could not be performed.\n#### 3.2.4 Comparison with similar algorithms\n\nL-Town is a widely used benchmark model in WDN research, originally introduced by BattLeDIM. Accordingly, numerous studies have evaluated leakage-detection algorithms on this benchmark. Daniel et al. (2022) employed a two-stage framework combining sequential detection and model-based localization; a semi-supervised 1D-CNN autoencoder was used to reconstruct pressure signals for leak localization(Tornyeviadzi & Seidu, 2023); a graph-based state interpolation (GSI) was performed to complete hydraulic states and applied dictionary-learning (DL) classification, achieving localization from pressure series sampled at 5-min intervals(Irofti et al., 2022); Ma et al. (2022) combined SCADA-driven state estimation with real-time demand calibration and history–simulation differencing for leakage detection; Wan et al. (2023) used an Artificial Neural Network (ANN) for multi-step forecasting of window-level future flows, employing cosine-distance residuals with EWMA smoothing to suppress noise and set leakage-alarm thresholds.\n\nAnalytical methods also constitute a mainstream family of approaches for leakage identification. As early as 2016, Delgado-Aguiñaga et al. (2016) proposed multi-leak diagnosis in pipelines based on extended Kalman filter; the core of such methods is to analyze sustained, high-resolution pressure series and detect leaks by contrasting features between leakage and normal states. The driving paradigm of these methods is not aligned with that used in the present approach. Because the two study datasets employed here (the simulated WDN and L-Town) do not contain the high-resolution pressure series required by those methods, an effective head-to-head comparison could not be performed. A coarse, mechanism-level comparison is still possible: only a sampling rate of 100 Hz is reported, and for typical problem sizes the computational cost is on the order of 5–80 Million Floating-point Operations Per second (MFLOPs). In this study, under the binary classification setting (c = 1), the two-layer GCN has a single forward-pass computational complexity of $$O( {nfh + eh + nhc + ec + nh + nc} )$$. Converting each multiply–accumulate into 2 Floating-point Operations Per second (FLOPs) and substituting the graph size used in this work (n = 34, e = 44, f = 2, and h = 64), the single-sample inference cost is approximately 0.0232 MFLOPs. Considering a constant factor for backpropagation and parameter updates of alpha ≈3, one training step per graph is approximately 0.0696 FLOPs. Although longer time is consumed during training, inference for a single sample is at the millisecond level once the model has been trained. The referenced work does not analyze noise robustness; its sensor data indicate a maximum relative pressure error of 5%, and, if 10% Gaussian noise were also injected, its precision would likely fall below that of the present algorithm.\n\nIt should be emphasized that all six localization methods are primarily driven by long time-series hydraulic features, thereby enabling pipe-level precise localization.\n\nIn contrast to the above driving paradigm, supervised CNN and MLP models (Basnet et al., 2023) were employed for leak localization in L-Town. These studies systematically examined scenarios of increasing complexity—no noise, demand noise, mixed noise, and random leak—to localize the leakage district in L-Town. The findings indicate that leakage in WDN can be effectively identified when given the close similarity of their modeling paradigm and research objective to those of the present work, subsequent comparisons in this section were focused on these two models (CNN and MLP). Multi-source inputs (pressure plus demand) are used.\n\nRobustness analyses for CNN and MLP were performed by adding Gaussian noise to demand and pressure data. Their no-noise setting corresponds to the single-sensor, full-feature simulated network used in this study. The demand-noise setting adds 10% Gaussian noise to demands while retaining simulated, noise-free pressures. This is analogous to Section 3.1.3 in which demand data with 20% Gaussian noise and exact pressures were used as inputs. Accordingly, outputs from the single-sensor model in Section 3.1.3 were used for comparison with the demand-noise case. positive–negative logit gaps, producing high‐confidence predictions near 0 or 1. However, as input noise increases and the feature signal‐to‐noise ratio declines, the final‐layer logits tend toward zero, causing outputs to cluster near 0.5. In combination with the asymmetric compression of negative logits by LeakyReLU, the model ultimately tends to classify samples as normal (0) in high‐noise regimes.\n\nOverall, within the attainable error range of pressure sensors in WDN, the model performance remains essentially stable; once noise exceeds 0.5–1.0 m, accuracy degrades and FNR increases markedly. The model's performance on real WDN data (L‐Town) will be discussed in Section 3.2.\n\n### Experiments of WDN in L‐Town\n\nAs discussed in Section 3.1 on small‐scale simulated networks, GCN can be effectively coupled with WDN and used for abnormal state recognition and pipe burst region identification. In this chapter, GCN is integrated with the L‐Town network to conduct modeling experiments on large‐scale networks.\n\n#### Introduction of the L‐Town WDN\n\nL‐Town is a benchmark model widely used in WDN research, initially introduced by BattLeDIM (Dataset of BattLeDIM: Battle of the Leakage Detection & Isolation Methods, 2020). The model provides a realistic yet anonymized WDN, along with extensive historical and evaluation datasets, offering valuable resources for researchers and engineers in the development and assessment of leakage detection and isolation methods.\n\nThe L‐Town dataset includes detailed EPANET model files, comprising nominal parameters, actual network parameters, and historical baseline demands for nodes. In addition to the network model, the dataset provides extensive measurement data from the SCADA system, including: demand, including water consumption measurements from 82 automated meter reading devices; water‐level data, including tank water‐level measurements from 1 water‐level sensor; pressure data, including pressure measurements from 33 pressure sensors.\n\nIn addition, the dataset includes detailed information and time‐series data on leakage events, as well as reports on repaired leaks. These data provide a solid foundation for studying the operation of WDN, developing leakage detection algorithms, and evaluating system performance.\n\n#### Full‐scale WDN model\n\nThis section first conducts anomaly‐state recognition experiments on a full‐scale WDN. According to the L‐Town “readme” documentation: The nominal base demands for each node are based on average historical metered consumption. In general, the difference between the actual and the nominal values for each consumer type (residential and commercial) is less than 10%. However, with AdaBoost, we assign an *importance weight* $a_i$ to each classifier $C_i$, where $a_i>0$.\\r\\nanalysis/week7/11_Ensembles_and_Boosting.txt:118:## **11.5  AdaBoost Pseudocode<font color='#008DFF'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\nanalysis/week7/11_Ensembles_and_Boosting.txt:138:**Note 4:** AdaBoost requires that classifiers be trained sequentially, i.e. one at a time. However, when the ensemble is deployed, these classifiers can process the input independently, before their predictions are combined in the final voting step.\\r\\nanalysis/week7/11_Ensembles_and_Boosting.txt:142:## **11.6 Gradient Boosting<font color='#FFCC00'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\nanalysis/week7/11_Ensembles_and_Boosting.txt:144:Gradient Boosting is a more recent method for constructing ensembles of classifiers.
