Gradient Boosting.txt:2:Ensembles and Boosting\\r\\n../TranscriptClasses/Week 7/DS675.M11.V06. Gradient Boosting.txt:4:DS675.M11.V06. Gradient Boosting\\r\\n../TranscriptClasses/Week 7/DS675.M11.V06. Gradient Boosting.txt:27:This is gradient boosting.\\r\\n../TranscriptClasses/Week 7/DS675.M11.V06. Gradient Boosting.txt:29:In particular, there is a library that's called x z boost, which has been a key ingredient in multiple good and winning submissions on Google.\\r\\n../TranscriptClasses/Week 7/DS675.M11.V06. Gradient Boosting.txt:31:There are a number of mathematical details about how gradient boosting works and what we're going to do in this.\\r\\n../TranscriptClasses/Week 7/DS675.M11.V06. Gradient Boosting.txt:163:This is the fundamental idea in in grading, boosting these this idea of learning and correcting the residual atoms.\\r\\n**Note 3:** In the update formula for the weights, $b_t(X_i)$ is the label predicted for point $X_i$, and so $y_i b_t(X_i)$ can be either $1$ when the true and predicted labels are the same, or $-1$ otherwise. If classifier $b_t$ has a positive importance weight $a_t$, then when the true and predicted weights are different, the weight of point $i$ increases, and more attention will be given to it in the training of future classifiers. Otherwise, the weight decreases, and less attention will be given to it in future training. For classifiers that are \\\"untrustworthy\\\" ($a_t<0$), the effect on the importance weight of $X_i$ is *reversed*.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Note 4:** AdaBoost requires that classifiers be trained sequentially, i.e. one at a time. However, when the ensemble is deployed, these classifiers can process the input independently, before their predictions are combined in the final voting step.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 10 markdown\\r\\n## **11.6 Gradient Boosting<font color='#FFCC00'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\nGradient Boosting is a more recent method for constructing ensembles of classifiers. One of its implementations ([xgboost](https://xgboost.readthedocs.io/en/stable/)) has been widely used in Kaggle competitions, mostly by using [boosted trees](https://xgboost.readthedocs.io/en/stable/tutorials/model.html).\\r\\n\\r\\nThe basic idea behind gradient boosting is better understood in the context of regression. Suppose we have $n$ points and their numerical labels $(X_i,y_i)$ and we have learned some regression model $h_0$ that makes a prediction\\r\\n\\r\\n$$\\r\\n     \\\\hat{y}_i = h_0(X_i) \\\\, .\\r\\n$$\\r\\n\\r\\nNow, for every point $X_i$ we can calculate the residual error $r_i = y_i - \\\\hat{y}_i$. If the first model can be improved, then it is likely the case that there will be some type of 'pattern' in these residuals.\\r\\nWe can then try to add another model $h_1$ that will learn the residuals, i.e. design a regression model for the points $X_i$, with numerical labels $r_i$. The motivation is this: *if* we are able to learn a model $h_1$ such that $r_i = h_1(X_i)$, then we can completely correct the original model $h_0$, by using the 'composite' ensemble model:\\r\\n\\r\\n$$\\r\\n    E(x) = h_0(x) + h_1(x) \\\\, .\\r\\n$$\\r\\n\\r\\nOf course, $h_1$ is not expected to be a fully perfect model for the regression problem $(X,r_i)$, so $E(x)$ will still have some residual error that we can try to model with yet another model $h_2$, and the process can continue in this way for a number of rounds $T$.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Cell 11 markdown\\r\\n## **11.7  Ensembles and Boosting Notebook<font color='#369870'> &#x2B24; </font>**\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nHere is the [code notebook](https://colab.research.google.com/drive/1FZu1VuMlAyNe06s5wLP_kPQr8dQU1fGn?usp=drive_fs#scrollTo=9c7qu-xKcCX3).\\r\\n\"}"

Prediction of daily water consumption in residential areas based on meteorologic conditions‐applying gradient boosting regression tree algorithm\n\n## Computer-Aided Civil and Infrastructure Engineering\n\nVolume 40, Issue 31, 29 December 2025, Pages 6656-6677\n\nRESEARCH ARTICLE\n\nDetection and localization of pipeline leakage of water distribution networks based on graph convolutional networks\n\nhttps://doi.org/10.1111/mice.70173Get rights and content\n\nUnder a Creative Commons license\n\nOpen access\n\n## Abstract\n\nA leak in a water distribution network (WDN) creates a localized pressure depression whose effect diffuses along the network topology through headloss coupling. This spatial diffusion is well approximated by graph Laplacian smoothing, so we use a graph convolutional network as a learnable surrogate of hydraulic information propagation on the graph. The method casts single‐snapshot leakage analysis as graph‐level classification of normal versus abnormal states using topology‐aware convolution. Localization then leverages gradient‐based node importance—sensitivities of the leak logit to node features—mapped to pipes via linear interpolation to produce leakage‐risk contour maps. The importance of node i is defined in Equation (7).7{S}_{i}=\\sum _{k}\\left|\\frac{\\partial o}{\\partial {x}_{i,k}}\\right|where {S}_{i} is the importance of node i; {x}_{i,k} is the kth feature of node i; and \\frac{\\partial o}{\\partial {x}_{i,k}} is the gradient of the output o with respect to the kth feature of node i.\n\nThis gradient, obtained via backpropagation, quantifies how local perturbations of node features—after propagating through the graph—affect the leakage‐related output. Since message passing in GCNs is mathematically equivalent to Laplacian smoothing on the adjacency structure, \\frac{\\partial o}{\\partial {x}_{i,k}} can be regarded as a sensitivity propagation coefficient for leakage response diffusing along edges; accordingly, the node importance characterizes both the spatial extent and the magnitude of a local hydraulic disturbance's impact on the overall system.\n\nBecause leakage occurs on pipe segments rather than at nodes, node‐level importance is mapped to edges. From a hydraulic perspective, pressure varies continuously along a pipe and, under small perturbations, can be linearized using the Hazen–Williams relation, so inter‐nodal responses are treated as linearly transmitted along the segment. The arrangement of these pressure monitoring points and convergence curves is illustrated in Figure 7.\n\nImage: FIGURE 7\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (83KB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFIGURE 7. Convergence curves and arrangement of pressure monitoring points.\n\nFour groups of data were observed, and it was found that the reduction in the number of monitoring points impacts the model's convergence. However, a single monitoring point is still sufficient to support effective model convergence.\n\nIn Figure 7a, the configuration with nine pressure sensors performs best: the loss decreases smoothly from 0.6869 to a final value of 0.0216, while ACC rises from 50% to 99.1%; the curves are very smooth, indicating strong convergence. By contrast, in Figure 7b (four pressure sensors), the loss curve exhibits mild mid‐course fluctuations but maintains a clear downward trajectory, converging to 0.0214 approximately; ACC reaches 98.7%, slightly lower than Figure 7a yet still indicative of stable performance. This could be attributed to the presence of only one pressure monitoring point, as the remaining demand monitoring points cannot provide direct pressure information. Since the GCN model relies on adjacency relationships in the graph structure for information propagation, the use of a single pressure monitoring point limits the extent to which information can spread in the graph. Consequently, the information from this node can only be effectively transmitted to neighboring nodes, while non‐monitored nodes and demand monitoring points fail to supplement sufficient pressure data. This directly impacts the model's overall predictive ability for node pressures, causing the gradient descent speed to gradually slow down. Nevertheless, after 50 epochs, the final CE loss value decreases to approximately 0.0452. The ACC reaches 97.1%. Although its performance is slightly inferior to conditions with multiple pressure monitoring points, it still converges effectively and supports the model's stability.\n\nFigure 8 presents the algorithm's ACC, FNR, and FPR under different numbers of pressure monitoring points. \nhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.measurement.2024.116013\n\nGoogle Scholar\n\n  7. DOI: 10.1061/JWRMD5.WRENG-6001.© 2023 American Society\n\nof Civil Engineers.\n\nIntroduction\n\nPipeline leakage is likely to have several negative influences on\n\nwater distribution networks (WDNs): (1) enormous economic loss\n\n(Wan et al. 2022), (2) wasted energy consumption from pumping\n\n(Puust et al. 2010), and (3) water quality contamination by intro-\n\nducing infection during hydraulic transients (Colombo et al. 2009).\n\nTherefore, the problem of detecting and resolving leaks is of great\n\nconcern to water companies and authorities. Multistep forecasting\n\nis a challenging extension of traditional one-step forecasting. When\n\nthe prediction horizon is extended from a single point to a time\n\nwindow, various problems will be introduced to the forecasting,\n\nsuch as error accumulation, reduced accuracy, and increased uncer-\n\ntainty (Ben Taieb et al. 2012). Artificial neural networks (ANN),\n\nwhich provide direct support for multistep forecasting, are adopted\n\nin this paper for predicting multiple steps forward.\n\nIn the proposed methodology, the observed flow data is pre-\n\ndicted in the long-term using the ANN model first. Next, the com-\n\nparison between the observed vector and the predicted vector is\n\ncalculated based on cosine distance. Finally, the EWMA is used\n\nto smooth residual vectors to reduce the influence of noises and\n\nuncertainties. A threshold is set for the smoothed residual vectors\n\nand raises alarms during leakage events. The proposed method is\n\napplied to a case study called L-Town which contained one year\n\nof real-life flow monitoring data in an online manner. The results\n\ndemonstrate the applicability of the proposed method for gradual\n\nleakage detection and proves its effectiveness by detecting small\n\ngradual leakage events within just a few days while generating no\n\nfalse alarms. The proposed method doesn’t require information\n\nsuch as smart meter readings and hydraulic models that are usually\n\nnot widespread in water companies.\n\nMethodology\n\nThe traditional one-step prediction models accurately detect burst\n\nevents characterised by abrupt changes in monitoring datasets\n\n(decreasing in pressure and increasing in flow) but miss the scenario\n\nthat some leakage events gradually develop over time. This paper\n\nproposes a novel methodology for detecting gradual leakage events\n\nWhen an alarm is raised during a leakage event, the leakage\n\nevent could be regarded as correctly identified. In order to further\n\nevaluate the accuracy of the detection methodology, the DT is\n\nused to evaluate the effectiveness of a leakage detection method,\n\nespecially for gradual leakage detection. As gradual leakage\n\nevents will always become apparent, provided enough time has\n\npassed for the rate of leakage to build up. The detection time\n\nis defined as the elapsed time from the start of a leakage event\n\nto the time when the event is first detected. Detection time reflects\n\nhow quickly the leakage detection algorithm responds, and the\n\nvolume of water saved.\n\nSynthetic Case Study\n\nDescription of Study Area\n\nThe case study L-Town WDN was provided by the organizers of\n\nBattLeDIM (Vrachimis et al. 2020). The graphical representation\n\nof L-Town is represented in Fig. 4. This hydraulic model is based\n\non a real WDN located in Cyprus with about 10,000 consumers.\n\nThis L-Town WDN consists of 905 pipes with a total length of\n\n42.6 km, and 782 junctions. Two reservoirs are responsible for pro-\n\nviding clean water with a pressure head of at least 20 m to all water\n\nconsumers. As shown in Fig. 4, L-Town can be divided into three\n\ndistinct hydraulic areas: (1) Area A, which is the main area and\n\nreceives water from two reservoirs, (2) Area B, located downstream\n\nof Area A, a pressure reduction valve (PRV) has been installed to\n\nreduce the water pressure in Area B, and (3) Area C, located in an\n\narea with higher elevation, and receiving water from a water tank\n\nlocated between Area A and Area C.\n\nThere are three types of consumers in L-Town, which are res-\n\nidential, commercial and industrial. All consumers’behavior pat-\n\nterns are well-modeled in the hydraulic model with a sampling rate\n\nof 5 min, allowing researchers to have a more reliable and realistic\n\ntool for generating monitoring data and testing the ability of leak-\n\nage detection methods. In this paper, only flow data at the exits of\n\nwater resources are recorded. As represented by the red triangle in\n2022;Marzola et al. 2022). Therefore, the original\n\ndataset has been changed to suit the single leakage assumption\n\nof the proposed method. The real hydraulic model published by\n\nVrachimis et al. (2022) is used in this paper to generate a monitor-\n\ning dataset to maintain the uncertainties caused by time-varying\n\nconsumer demand patterns.\n\nSimulation of Gradual Leakages\n\nWater network tool for resilience (WNTR) (Klise et al. 2017)is\n\nused in this paper to simulate gradual leakage events in the L-Town.\n\nLeakages are modeled by splitting the pipe into two sections and\n\nadding a junction, and additional outflow is added to the leak\n\nFig. 5. The results\n\nshow that the single-step model detects the event when it reaches a\n\nrelatively large size at 16:45 on November 27, and the proposed\n\nMIMO model captured the growing trend in the dataset and detects\n\nthe leakage event at 15:00 on November 26, before the leakage be-\n\ncomes a burst event. This is because just after the leak-\n\nage events, the input vector cannot represent the normal situation of\n\nthe distribution system. Thus, the prediction model will continue to\n\ngenerate abnormal output until there are enough input data.\n\nThe third assumption is that the system operation condition\n\ndoesn’t change during detection. If the operation condition changed\n\nafter the model developed from historical data, a separate model\n\nneeds to be developed for the changed situation. For example, if\n\nthere is a pressure reduction valve installed in the system, the sys-\n\ntem behaviour will follow another pattern, and the old model can-\n\nnot be applied after the installation.\n\nConclusion\n\nIn this work, a method is proposed to detect gradual leakage events.\n\nThe proposed method contains two main stages: prediction and de-\n\ntection. In the prediction stage, a MIMO-ANN is trained on the\n\nhistorical dataset to model the system behaviour under healthy con-\n\nditions, and then the residual vector is obtained by comparing the\n\noutput vector with the observed measurements based on cosine dis-\n\ntance. In the classification stage, the EWMA is used to smooth the\n\nresiduals and eliminate spikes caused by noises. Then, leakage\n\ncould be detected by comparing the residual values against a\n\nuser-designated threshold. The proposed method has been tested\n\non the L-Town distribution network.\n\nThe reason why a multi-step forecasting approach is needed for\n\ngradual leakage detection has been illustrated. This is because the\n\nresidual vector obtained from the one-step prediction model doesn’t\n\ncontain any memory of the historical data. However, by extending\n\nthe prediction horizon, the multi-step model presented in this paper\n\nsuccessfully detected all the leakage events. The superiority of the\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nGraph neural networks to model and optimize the operation of Water Distribution Networks: A review (https://iris.uniroma1.it/retrieve/103004b5-4c01-4840-8a23-44817f38bd82/Vittori_Graph-neural-networks_2025.pdf)\nciteturn94search12 [wordlim: 200] Published: 1.3 years ago; Crawled: 1.3 years ago; The method relies on two main components: **reconstructor**, which retrieves nodal pressures for a specific instant using sparse pressure measurements, and **predictor**, which predicts future pressure measurements based on historical data. ... The methodology was validated on the **BattLeDIM [124]** dataset from the L-Town network.\nThe WDN topology is a graph of junctions and pipes, as described in Section 3.2.\n\nThe adjacency matrix A, capturing node connectivity, is constructed using three weighting schemes:\n\n- *unweighted edges:* A_uv = 1;\n- *pipe length weighted:* A_uv = l_uv with l_uv being the pipe length;\n- *hydraulic loss weighted:* A_uv = 4.727 · C_uv^1.852 · d_uv^4.871 / l_uv with reference to Hazen-Williams equation.\n\nAdditionally, self-loops were added to the graph to enhance the influence of pressure monitoring points in the signal reconstruction process.\n\nThe method relies on two main components: **reconstructor**, which retrieves nodal pressures for a specific instant using sparse pressure measurements, and **predictor**, which predicts future pressure measurements based on historical data. Let the observed pressure data at time t on the graph G be denoted as x_G^(t) = [x_1^(t), x_2^(t), ..., x_N^(t)], where most values are NaN, reflecting the limited number of pressure monitoring points. The reconstructor infers the missing pressures y_r^(t), and the predictor uses historical data over a time window T, denoted as:\n\nx_G^(t−T:t−1) = [x_G^(t−T), x_G^(t−T+1), ..., x_G^(t−1)],\n\nto predict the pressures at the next time step ŷ_p^(t).\n\nThe residual signal is defined as the difference between the predicted and reconstructed values:\n\nr(t) = ŷ_p^(t) − ŷ_r^(t).\n\nUnder normal (non-leak) conditions, the residual signal remains stationary, influenced only by random noise. A deviation from this behavior indicates the presence of a leak. The residual signal on edges to localize the leak is defined as:\n\nr_uv(t) = r_v(t) − r_u(t),\n\nwhere u and v are the nodes connected by an edge, a leakage is detected when the moving average of the residual, \\bar{r}_uv(t) = 1/m ∑_{i=0}^{m−1} r_uv(t − i), exceeds a threshold \\bar{ρ}_uv(t) = α \\bar{σ}_uv(t), where α controls the rate of false alarms and \\bar{σ}_uv(t) is the standard deviation over the window.\n\nTo ensure accurate localization, the authors applied a **Chebyshev spectral graph convolutional network** (Section 4.2.2), which preserves high-frequency (localized) information. For the reconstructor, the input channel size c_in is set to 1, while for the predictor, it is T, corresponding to the time window size.\n\nThe methodology was validated on the **BattLeDIM [124]** dataset from the L-Town network. The EPANET nominal model and sensor measurements from 2018 were used to train the reconstructor and predictor, with documented leak information (size and inflow) as ground truth. The evaluation employed two primary metrics: the Economic score (Euros saved), which reflects cost savings from early leak detection by reducing non-revenue water and repair costs, and the True\n\n12--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nOFR 2019–1076: Baseline Groundwater Hydrology and Water Quality In and Around Bluff, Utah (https://pubs.usgs.gov/of/2019/1076/ofr20191076.pdf)\nciteturn94search13 [wordlim: 200] Published: 7.2 years ago; Crawled: 7.2 years ago; By Olivia L. ... The Glen Canyon Group includes the Entrada, Navajo, and Wingate aquifers. The region has a long history of oil and gas development centered on the Aneth Oil Field to the east of Bluff that began in the mid-1950s. New hydrocarbon production wells have been proposed 5 to 7 miles (mi) northeast of Bluff, east of highway 191 (fig. 1). These wells will be drilled through formations from which the town’s municipal water supply is sourced and will be used for the injection of high-pressure fluids to hydraulically fracture deeper formations to enhance recovery. The new wells are expected to be completed at depths of 4,000 to 7,000 feet (ft). The methodology makes use of several Artificial Intelligence techniques including Wavelets for de-noising of the recorded pressure and/or flow signals, Artificial Neural Networks for the short-term forecasting of future pressure and/or flow signal values, Statistical Process Control analysis for the analysis of discrepancies between the predicted (i.e., expected) and the actually observed signal values, and finally, the Bayesian Network based inference system for classification of discrepancies and raising of alarms.\nNew and more efficient methodologies for the real-time detection of pipe bursts are required to detect these events thus enabling company personnel to react quickly and mitigate the negative impacts of the burst. This paper presents an on-line methodology for automated detection of leaks and bursts by analysing the data collected by real-time sensors. The methodology makes use of several Artificial Intelligence techniques including Wavelets for de-noising of the recorded pressure and/or flow signals, Artificial Neural Networks for the short-term forecasting of future pressure and/or flow signal values, Statistical Process Control analysis for the analysis of discrepancies between the predicted (i.e., expected) and the actually observed signal values, and finally, the Bayesian Network based inference system for classification of discrepancies and raising of alarms. A case study from a real-life DMA in the United Kingdom with simulated (i.e., engineered) burst events is discussed in this paper. The results obtained illustrate that the methodology presented has the potential to yield substantial improvements to the state-of-the-art in real-time water distribution system management by detecting leaks and pipe bursts as they occur in a fast and reliable manner. The results obtained also show that to analyse the discrepancies by using a set of anomaly identification rules applied to consecutive time steps in the Statistical Process Control analysis improves the efficiency and reliability of the real-time leak detection system.\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nMachine Learning in Water Management | PDF | Groundwater | Deep Learning (https://www.scribd.com/document/858050068/1-s2-0-S0959652624001628-main)\nciteturn94search5 [wordlim: 200] Published: 2.8 years ago; Crawled: 1.4 years ago; GBRT Gradient Boosted Regression Trees ... Physical-based models capture the underlying hydrological processes, while ML models handle complex, non-linear relationships in data. Studies like those of Kayhomayoon et al. (2022) used MODFLOW and ANFIS/ANN for effective regional groundwater level (GWL) prediction. These models are optimized using methods such as the Harris Hawks Optimization and Particle Swarm Optimization, highlighting their adaptability and enhanced accuracy in various environmental conditions .\n\n### \n\n## Which machine learning models were found more effective for groundwater level prediction, and what factors contribute to their effectiveness?\n\nRandom Forest (RF) and Gradient Boosting Regression (GBR) were shown to be effective in predicting groundwater levels (GWL). RF was notably better in one-step-ahead predictions of GWL in the space-time domain due to its ability to handle input parameter variability, like precipitation and evapotranspiration. GBR performed well with short- and long-lead times in the Rafsanjan aquifer due to higher water depth and abstraction rates yielding better prediction outcomes. Moreover, models like GRU and LSTM have been superior in regions with extreme climate events and more frequent baseflow interactions .\n\n### \n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nUntitled (https://ttabvue.uspto.gov/ttabvue/ttabvue-91216585-OPP-96.pdf)\nciteturn94search14 [wordlim: 200] Published: 9.5 years ago; Crawled: 7.7 years ago; At the center of Kate Spade’s whirlwind of success is a lightning rod named William L. ... See cite634†Glossary .\nL711: \nL712: max_features{‘sqrt’, ‘log2’}, int or float, default=None\nL713: The number of features to consider when looking for the best split:\nL714: \nL715:   * If int, values must be in the range `[1, inf)`.\nL716: \nL717:   * If float, values must be in the range `(0.0, 1.0]` and the features considered at each split will be `max(1, int(max_features * n_features_in_))`.\nL718: \nL719:   * If “sqrt”, then `max_features=sqrt(n_features)`.\nL720: \nL721:   * If “log2”, then `max_features=log2(n_features)`.\nL722: \nL723:   * If None, then `max_features=n_features`.\nL724: Choosing `max_features < n_features` leads to a reduction of variance and an increase in bias.\nL725: \nL726: Note: the search for a split does not stop until at least one valid partition of the node samples is found, even if it requires to effectively inspect more than `max_features` features.\nL727: \nL728: alpha float, default=0.9\nL729: \nL730: \nL731: The alpha-quantile of the huber loss function and the quantile loss function. Only if `loss='huber'` or `loss='quantile'`. Values must be in the range `(0.0, 1.0)`.\nL732: \nL733: verbose int, default=0\nL734: Enable verbose output. If 1 then it prints progress and performance once in a while (the more trees the lower the frequency). If greater than 1 then it prints progress and performance for every tree. Values must be in the range `[0, inf)`.\nL735: \nL736: max_leaf_nodes int, default=None\nL737: \nL738: \nL739: Grow trees with `max_leaf_nodes` in best-first fashion. Best nodes are defined as relative reduction in impurity. Values must be in the range `[2, inf)`. If None, then unlimited number of leaf nodes.\nL740: warm_start bool, default=False\nL741: \nL742: \nL743: When set to `True`, reuse the solution of the previous call to fit and add more estimators to the ensemble, otherwise, just erase the previous solution. See cite635†the Glossary .\nL744: \nL745: validation_fraction float, default=0.1\nL746: \nL747: \nL748: The proportion of training data to set aside as validation set for early stopping. Values must be in the range `(0.0, 1.0)`. Only used if `n_iter_no_change` is set to an integer.\nL749: \nL750: Added in version 0.20.\nL751: \nL752: n_iter_no_change int, default=None\nL753: `n_iter_no_change` is used to decide if early stopping will be used to terminate training when validation score is not improving. By default it is set to None to disable early stopping. If set to a number, it will set aside `validation_fraction` size of the training data as validation and terminate training when validation score is not improving in all of the previous `n_iter_no_change` numbers of iterations. Values must be in the range `[1, inf)`. See cite636†Early stopping in Gradient Boosting .\nL754: Added in version 0.20.\nL755: \nL756: tol float, default=1e-4\nL757: \nL758: \nL759: Tolerance for the early stopping. When the loss is not improving by at least tol for `n_iter_no_change` iterations (if set to a number), the training stops. Values must be in the range `[0.0, inf)`.\nL760: \nL761: Added in version 0.20.\nL762: \nL763: ccp_alpha non-negative float, default=0.0\nL764: Complexity parameter used for Minimal Cost-Complexity Pruning. The subtree with the largest cost complexity that is smaller than `ccp_alpha` will be chosen. By default, no pruning is performed. Values must be in the range `[0.0, inf)`. See cite637†Minimal Cost-Complexity Pruning for details. See cite638†Post pruning decision trees with cost complexity pruning for an example of such pruning.\nL765: \nL766: Added in version 0.22.\nL767: \nL768: Attributes:\nL769: \nL770: \nL771: n_estimators_int\nL772: The number of estimators as selected by early stopping (if `n_iter_no_change` is specified). Otherwise it is set to `n_estimators`.\nL773: \nL774: n_trees_per_iteration_int\nL775: \nL776: \nL777: The number of trees that are built at each iteration. For regressors, this is always 1.\nL778: \nL779: Added in version 1.4.0.\nL780: \nL781: `feature_importances_`ndarray of shape (n_features,)\nL782: \nL783: \nL784: The impurity-based feature importances.\nL785: \nL786: oob_improvement_ndarray of shape (n_estimators,)\nL787: The improvement in loss on the out-of-bag samples relative to the previous iteration. `oob_improvement_[0]` is the improvement in loss of the first stage over the `init` estimator. Only available if `subsample < 1.0`.\nL788: \nL789: oob_scores_ndarray of shape (n_estimators,)\nL790: \nL791: \nL792: The full history of the loss values on the out-of-bag samples. Only available if `subsample < 1.0`.\nL793: \nL794: Added in version 1.3.\nL795: \nL796: oob_score_float\nL797: The last value of the loss on the out-of-bag samples. It is the same as `oob_scores_[-1]`. Only available if `subsample < 1.0`.\nL798: \nL799: Added in version 1.3.\nL800: \nL801: train_score_ndarray of shape (n_estimators,)\nL802: \nL803: \nL804: The i-th score `train_score_[i]` is the loss of the model at iteration `i` on the in-bag sample. If `subsample == 1` this is the loss on the training data.\nL805: \nL806: init_estimator\nL807: \nL808: \nL809: The estimator that provides the initial predictions. Set via the `init` argument.\nL810: estimators_ndarray of DecisionTreeRegressor of shape (n_estimators, 1)\nL811: \nL812: \nL813: The collection of fitted sub-estimators.\nL814: \nL815: n_features_in_int\nL816: \nL817: \nL818: Number of features seen during cite632†fit .\nL819: \nL820: Added in version 0.24.\nL821: \nL822: feature_names_in_ndarray of shape (`n_features_in_`,)\nL823: \nL824: \nL825: Names of features seen during cite632†fit . Therefore, the best found split may vary, even with the same training data and `max_features=n_features`, if the improvement of the MSE is identical for several splits enumerated during the search of the best split. To obtain a deterministic behaviour during fitting, `random_state` has to be fixed.\nL851: \nL852: References\nL853: \nL854: J.
